To check (5)

To check Non-Significant P Value Check

What we found

We found 1 non-significant p value that should be checked for appropriate interpretation.

Show details

Meta-scientific research has shown nonsignificant p values are commonly misinterpreted. It is incorrect to infer that there is ‘no effect’, ‘no difference’, or that groups are ‘the same’ after p > 0.05.

It is possible that there is a true non-zero effect, but that the study did not detect it. Make sure your inference acknowledges that it is possible that there is a non-zero effect. It is correct to include the effect is ‘not significantly’ different, although this just restates that p > 0.05.

Metacheck does not yet analyze automatically whether sentences which include non-significant p-values are correct, but we recommend manually checking the sentences below for possible misinterpreted non-significant p values.

Text Sentence
P = 0.69 On identical CLEF batches, Haiku barely changed (0.870 vs 0.862; P = 0.69).

For metascientific articles demonstrating the rate of misinterpretations of non-significant results is high, see:

Aczel B, Palfi B, Szollosi A, Kovacs M, Szaszi B, Szecsi P, Zrubka M, Gronau Q, van den Bergh D, Wagenmakers E (2018). “Quantifying Support for the Null Hypothesis in Psychology: An Empirical Investigation.” Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science, 1(3), 357–366. doi:10.1177/2515245918773742.

Murphy S, Merz R, Reimann L, Fernández A (2025). “Nonsignificance misinterpreted as an effect’s absence in psychology: Prevalence and temporal analyses.” Royal Society Open Science, 12(3), 242167. doi:10.1098/rsos.242167.

For educational material on preventing the misinterpretation of p values, see Improving Your Statistical Inferences.

To check Codebook Check

What we found

  • We parsed 19 variable definitions from codebook/README files.
  • 0 of 50 data columns (0%) are documented in a codebook; 50 are not.
  • A codebook was found and parsed (19 variable definitions), but its variable names do not match the data columns, so the documentation could not be linked automatically (only 0% of columns matched). For example, the codebook documents stage`, `review`, `run, uid, record_id`, `id_type while the data has phase, agent, variant. This often means the data holds computed scores or subscales while the codebook lists the underlying items. To make the codebook usable automatically, document variables under the exact names used in the data file (one row per data column), with a label and, for rating items, the response scale.
Show details

This module checks whether each extracted data column is documented in a codebook or README, and flags documented variables that never appear in the data.

We examined 1 codebook/README/label source and 50 data columns across 4 files.

A codebook was found and parsed (19 variable definitions), but its variable names do not match the data columns, so the documentation could not be linked automatically (only 0% of columns matched). For example, the codebook documents stage`, `review`, `run, uid, record_id`, `id_type while the data has phase, agent, variant. This often means the data holds computed scores or subscales while the codebook lists the underlying items. To make the codebook usable automatically, document variables under the exact names used in the data file (one row per data column), with a label and, for rating items, the response scale.

README Files

README files are a way to document the contents and structure of a folder, helping users locate the information they need. You can use a README to document changes to a repository, and explain how files are named. Please consider adding a README to each repository or including ‘README’ in the name of your overview document.

Registration’s Source Project Is Closed

1 OSF source project is closed. The files metacheck lists below were not read from it — they were retrieved from the OSF registration’s own copy, which is separate from and unaffected by the project’s access setting. Nothing is currently lost, but this is still worth fixing: a closed source project means the files only survive for as long as the registration’s copy does, and anyone who follows the project link itself (rather than the registration) will find it inaccessible.

  • https://osf.io/svugd is closed; its files were retrieved from the OSF registration https://osf.io/zakg9.

Consider making the source project openly available.

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. No setwd() calls were found in any of the code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). No install.packages() calls were found in any of the code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In 11 code files, libraries/imports were at multiple places (i.e., with more than 3 non-comment lines in between).

Packages / Dependencies

The code files load 41 distinct packages/libraries: anthropic, argparse, ast, calendar, collections, concurrent, csv, datetime, docx, fcntl, fractions, glob, gzip, hashlib, importlib, io, itertools, json, math, matplotlib, multiprocessing, numpy, os, PIL, platform, random, re, runpy, scipy, shutil, sintesis, subprocess, sys, threading, time, unicodedata, urllib, validar_corpus, warnings, xml, zipfile. These are the names found in the source (no version information is available from static analysis).

Reproducible Environment

No list of the Python packages the code needs (a requirements.txt, environment.yml, Pipfile.lock, poetry.lock, uv.lock or pyproject.toml) was found anywhere in the repository. Without one, the package versions used for the analysis are not recoverable, and they may drift between when this analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt. Consider depositing a requirements.txt that lists each package with its version (for example the output of pip freeze).

Parsable code

All R-type code files (.R, .Rmd, .qmd) could be read in. There were no parsing issues.

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 4 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

File paths

44 data paths in the code do not work in the repository as deposited. We pointed them to where the file is, so the code could run: {R}/prospectivo/SUBMUESTRA_FRONTERA.json to docs/1_derivation_synergy/SUBMUESTRA_FRONTERA.json (in build_package_armar_paquete.py); {R}/analisis/26_cribado_openrouter.py to code/analisis/26_cribado_openrouter.py (in build_package_armar_paquete.py); {R}/datos/prevalencia2.json to ../../results/prevalencia2.json (in generar_apendice.py); {R}/datos/opcion3.json to ../../results/opcion3.json (in generar_apendice.py); {R}/datos/multifamilia.json to ../../results/multifamilia.json (in generar_apendice.py); {R}/datos/replica2.json to ../../results/replica2.json (in generar_apendice.py); datos/bitacora_agentes.jsonl to ../logs/bitacora_agentes.jsonl (in correr_agentes.py); {B}/datos/bitacora_anthropic.jsonl to ../../logs/bitacora_anthropic.jsonl (in sim_agentes.py); {B}/prospectivo/COHERENCIA_VEREDICTO.json to ../../docs/1_derivation_synergy/COHERENCIA_VEREDICTO.json (in sim_agentes.py); {B}/datos/resultados_empiricos.json to ../../results/resultados_empiricos.json (in 04_modelo.py), and 34 more. The code should be changed, because as written it does not find these files. The code reads files that are only inside ensemble_screening_llm_data_v1.zip, and no script unpacks it. We unpacked it in its folder before the run. So that others can run the code as deposited, start the code with a line that unpacks it (such as zipfile.ZipFile("ensemble_screening_llm_data_v1.zip").extractall() in Python), or upload the files unpacked. 29 references matched several files sharing a name and could not be resolved by study group or folder; they were left unrewritten and flagged.

Execution

The Python code was run. Each script ran in an isolated Docker container against a throwaway copy of the repository, in the run order above, with a 300-second per-script timeout. Of 73 scripts: 2 ran without error, 60 errored, 0 timed out, 6 could not run because one of its own dependencies is unavailable, 0 did not parse, and 0 are written for Python 2 and not run. Running against current package versions: a break can reflect version drift, which argues for pinning versions (e.g. in a requirements.txt).

Dependency-unavailable errors

6 scripts could not run because their own dependency is unavailable — the package failed to pip install. This is an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so it does not force the traffic light red.

File
14_busqueda.py
21_preparar_corridas.py
32_preparar_corridas_clef.py
41_preparar_corridas_r2.py
48_preparar_corridas_r3.py
53_preparar_corridas_op3.py

os.chdir() in the code

5 scripts call os.chdir(). This is bad practice: it hardcodes where the code must run (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine) and breaks the moment the code is run anywhere else. To run the code we replaced these calls with pass. Any such script that then ran is reproducible only after ignoring an os.chdir() that should not have been in the code and needs to be fixed.

File os.chdir() removed Path(s) it set
21_preparar_corridas.py 1 (non-literal)

More detail

Ethics Approval Check

The paper does not appear to report data about individual people or animals (we found: theorems or proofs), so we do not expect an ethics statement.

Ethics statement:

Ethics approval was not required because the study used published bibliographic records and review decisions and involved no human participants.

Power Analysis Check

No power analyses were detected.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.
